PBSCIENCE | PLANETARY HEALTH CHECK 2026                                                                                       1
Glossary
Planetary Boundary /      The science-based guardrails that show how far humanity can push the plan-
Boundaries (PB/PBs)       et’s natural systems while still keeping Earth stable, resilient, and able to sup-
                          port human wellbeing. The PB framework identifies nine major Earth system
                          processes that help keep the Planet in a relatively stable state. For each pro-
                          cess, scientists define a boundary: a level of human pressure that should not
                          be exceeded. Breaching these thresholds does not mean immediate disaster,
                          but it does mean that the risk for serious harm increases.

Safe Operating Space      The precautionary zone for human activity in which pressures on the Earth
                          system remain within Planetary Boundaries. Staying within this space ensures
                          Earth remains stable and supportive of life, and reduces the risk of crossing
                          harmful large-scale tipping points.

Zone of Increasing Risk   The space just beyond a Planetary Boundary, where human pressure on an
                          Earth system process is no longer considered within safe limits. In this zone,
                          risks to Earth system stability, resilience, and life-support functions grow as
                          pressure increases.

High Risk Zone            When conditions worsen beyond the Zone of Increasing Risk, they enter the
                          High Risk Zone. Here, the risk of severe, irreversible damage to key planetary
                          functions becomes not just possible, but likely. In this zone, immediate action
                          becomes critical to prevent locking in permanent changes.

Control Variable (CV)     The measures (or proxies) used to check whether an Earth system process is
                          staying within its Safe Operating Space (Planetary Boundary). Usually, scien-
                          tists track one or two control variables per boundary. For instance, atmospheric
                          CO2 concentration is a control variable for climate change.

Earth System              The set of interacting processes and components that determine how our
                          Planet functions. It includes the atmosphere, oceans, land, ice, ecosystems,
                          and human activities, all of which influence one another in complex ways. From
                          climate and ocean circulation to biodiversity and the use of natural resources,
                          these connections shape the conditions that support life and determine Earth’s
                          future stability.

Tipping Point,            A tipping point is a threshold in the Earth system where small, gradual pres-
Tipping Element           sures can trigger large changes, which are often impossible to reverse, and
                          which can come on quite suddenly. Once crossed, self‑reinforcing feedbacks
                          push the system further away from its previous state. For example, melting ice
                          exposes less reflective ocean water, which absorbs more sunlight and accel-
                          erates melting, creating a self-reinforcing cycle. Parts of the Earth system that
                          feature such a tipping point, such as the Amazon rainforest or Greenland ice
                          sheet, are called “tipping elements”.

Resilience                The capacity to absorb pressures, shocks, or disturbances while continuing to
                          function. In the Earth science context, this term is used to describe the abil-
                          ity of ecosystems, other parts of the Earth system, or the Planet as a whole
                          to cope with change and disturbance without losing their essential functions,
                          structure, or ability to recover.

Feedback                  A feedback occurs when the effects of a change influence how that change
                          develops. A positive feedback amplifies and reinforces the original change,
                          while a negative feedback counteracts and weakens it. Here, the terms “pos-
                          itive” and “negative” describe the direction of the effect, not whether the out-
                          come is judged to be good or bad. In the Earth system, feedbacks play an
                          important role in either accelerating change or promoting stability.




2                                                                   PBSCIENCE | PLANETARY HEALTH CHECK 2026
5 Key
Take-Aways                            1.	The impacts of transgressing Planetary Boundaries are becoming increas-
                                          ingly clear all around us. Results published in the last year show that climate
                                          change is progressing faster than previously expected, causing unprecedented
                                          heatwaves on land and in the ocean and fueling destructive extreme weather
                                          events. Tropical coral reefs have experienced their most severe mass bleach-
                                          ing event to date, a shutdown of the main Atlantic overturning circulation is
                                          now considered a credible risk even under low- to medium-emission scenar-
                                          ios, and major Antarctic ice masses may have already begun their slide into the
                                          ocean. With seven out of nine boundaries transgressed, we have pushed Earth
                                          outside of its Safe Operating Space – meaning that we are turning Earth from
                                          a safe home into one full of hazards that threaten people across the world.

                                      2.	Protecting and restoring nature is necessary to have a chance of a safe cli-
                                          mate landing for humanity. In the recent past, half of our CO2 emissions have
                                          been absorbed by the ocean and nature on land. When scientists chart a path
                                          towards a manageable climate future, their calculations generally assume that
                                          forests, wetlands, and other ecosystems will continue to function as “carbon
                                          sinks” in this way. But today, the land carbon sink is already showing signs
                                          of weakening and stagnation. A new study has confirmed that even a small
                                          amount of additional deforestation in the Amazon would likely push the region
                                          past a tipping point under current climate projections, resulting in the loss of
                                          two-thirds of the remaining forest. The only way to bring Earth below 1.5°C of
                                          global warming is to phase out fossil fuels and, simultaneously, to reverse the
                                          transgression of the other Planetary Boundaries – especially by safeguarding
                                          the integrity of the biosphere.

                                      3.	We have to protect the seafloor. The seafloor ecosystems play a significant role
                                          in the Earth system, yet we are destroying them at an alarming rate. Fishing tech-
                                          niques like bottom trawling produce long-lasting damage: Every year, 1-2% of the
                                          seafloor is trawled, with some of the most productive and diverse ocean regions
                                          being targeted most frequently. In addition, nascent seafloor mining activities are
                                          a looming threat to previously untouched regions of the deep ocean, with cur-
                                          rently incalculable impacts on the Earth system. More effective mechanisms to
                                          protect these vital but underexplored ecosystems are urgently needed.

                                      4.	Microplastics, forever chemicals, and other chemical pollutants present both
                                          known and yet-unknown dangers. For example, (micro-)plastics contaminate
                                          the whole Planet today, from mountaintops to the deep sea, and forever chem-
                                          icals like PFASs will not disappear from our environment for millennia. But this is
                                          only the tip of the iceberg: Humans are producing tens of thousands of chemi-
                                          cals and mixtures at unprecedented and increasing rates. We are already start-
                                          ing to see the harm these substances cause, from toxic effects on individual
                                          organisms to the disruption of Earth system processes. Even more dangerous
                                          are those effects that we can currently only guess at. Most of the substances
                                          we produce and release are not, or not sufficiently, tested for their impacts on
                                          the Earth system. We are flying blind: By the time we have a full picture of the
                                          danger, it might be too late to avoid it.

                                      5.	We still have a chance to turn towards a safer, sustainable future. The impacts
                                          of breached Planetary Boundaries are already all around us, and they will con-
                                          tinue to become more severe. But today, we still have a narrow window of
                                          opportunity. If we manage to bend the curves within this decade, we can put
                                          our societies on a course towards a safer, more just future. We have the tools,
                                          and we have taken steps in this direction, but our current efforts are far from
                                          sufficient. Only by pursuing urgent, ambitious, and decisive action across all
                                          sectors of our societies, can we return to living within the Safe Operating Space
                                          on a healthy Planet.



PBSCIENCE | PLANETARY HEALTH CHECK 2026                                                                                   3
About This Report
TITLE ARTICLE




The annual Planetary Health Check presents the most up-to-date assessment
of the state of the Planet. This year, for the first time, we are publishing this
report in two versions. You are currently reading the full-length version, pre-
senting the science of human-induced planetary changes in depth. The report
includes an introduction to the Planetary Boundaries framework and related
topics, such as tipping points and extreme weather events; three spotlight
chapters, each exploring a specific element or process of the Earth system in
more detail; and chapters for each of the nine Planetary Boundaries, compris-
ing the latest data, relevant definitions, and information on drivers, impacts,
and areas of current research.

For readers looking for the most important facts in a more condensed and acces-
sible form, we recommend exploring the Summary Version of the Planetary
Health Check, available at planetaryhealthcheck.org.




                                                                                                                                                     Nishant Rajput, www.pexels.com




  THE DYNAMIC PLANETARY HEALTH CHECK SYMBOL represents the summary of each year’s findings. The
stylized boxplot (white lines and blue dot) describes the distribution of all PB control variables. The
thin horizontal line represents the full range of all control variable values, while the thicker line repre-
sents the range that half of all control variable values fall into. The blue dot represents the median
of all control variables

  PLANETARY HEALTH AT A GLANCE. Just as a blood test provides insights into a human body’s health and identifies areas of concern, this Planetary Health
Check evaluates the 13 assessed control variables across the 9 Planetary Boundary (PB) processes to report on Earth’s stability, resilience, and life‑sup-
port functions – the overall health of our Planet. The 2026 assessment shows that seven of the nine PBs have been breached: Climate Change, Change
in Biosphere Integrity, Land System Change, Freshwater Change, Modification of Biogeochemical Flows, Introduction of Novel Entities, and Ocean Aci-
dification. All of these show worsening trends, suggesting further deterioration in the near future. Two PB processes remain within the Safe Operating
Space: Increase in Atmospheric Aerosol Loading (improving global trend) and Stratospheric Ozone Depletion (currently stable). The Planetary Health
Check Symbol ( FIG. 5) summarizes all of these findings, showing the Planet’s overall health at a glance.

    FIGURE 1: Humanity’s journey on Earth – Global temperature and the Great Acceleration from 500,000 years before present (BP) un-
 til 2100. The figure is a composite of different data sets including paleo data estimates, recent measurements, and future projections. For a
 full detailed description of this figure, please see Supplementary Materials. Data from: Jouzel et al. 2007,1 Masson-Delmotte et al. 2010,2 Os-
 man et al. 2021,3 PAGES 2k Consortium 2019,4 Morice et al. 2021,5 CRU 2026,6 IPCC Summary for Policymakers 2021,7 Steffen et al. 2015.8
 Key takeaway: For over 10,000 years, humanity lived in a very stable climatic period (the green corridor) in which human societies developed and
 adapted their technologies and cultures. By transgressing several Planetary Boundaries, including the one for Climate Change, we are moving beyond
 Holocene-like conditions and entering a new and dangerous terrain in which a rapidly changing global society must safeguard human well-being.

ocean acidification, and biodiversity decline. These coupled trends, which accelerated markedly after 1950, characterize the Great Acceleration and
are a defining feature of the Anthropocene, highlighting the intensifying pressure humans place on the Earth system. Although a few indicators, such as
marine fish capture, domesticated land, and stratospheric ozone depletion, show signs of flattening or recovery, most trends continue to rise steeply.
Together, these trajectories reinforce the urgency of respecting the Planetary Boundaries to safeguard Earth’s resilience.

THE NINE PLANETARY BOUNDARIES




1.3           The Nine Planetary Boundaries

Nine processes have been scientifically identified as key         called “control variables”. The colors (green for “within the
in regulating the stability, resilience, and life-support func-   Safe Operating Space”, yellow for “Zone of Increasing Risk”,
tions of the Earth system; these are known as the Planetary       and red for “High Risk Zone”) refer to the state of boundary
Boundaries (PBs) processes. To describe the state of these        transgression; SEE ● FIG. 4. To date, the PBs framework uses
9 processes, PBs assessments use representative variables,        1–2 control variables per PB (SEE ● TABLES IN CH. 7).



                                         1 Climate Change
                                         	The alteration of Earth’s radiative balance, for example by accumulating green-
                                           house gases in the atmosphere, affects global temperatures and climate patterns.

                                         2 Change in Biosphere Integrity
                                         	The decline in the diversity, extent, and health of living organisms and eco-
                                           systems affects the biosphere’s ability to co-regulate the state of the Planet
                                           by impacting the energy balance and chemical cycles on Earth.

                                         3 Land System Change
                                         	The transformation of natural landscapes, such as through deforestation and
                                           urbanization, diminishes ecological functions like carbon sequestration, mois-
                                           ture recycling, and habitats for wildlife, all crucial for Earth system health.

                                         4 Freshwater Change
                                         	The alteration of freshwater cycles, including rivers and soil moisture, leads
                                           to impacts on natural functions such as carbon sequestration and biodiver-
                                           sity, and potential shifts in future precipitation levels.

                                         5 Modification of Biogeochemical Flows
                                         	The disruption of natural nutrient cycles, such as nitrogen and phosphorus,
                                           affects land biodiversity and water quality, potentially triggering dead zones
                                           in freshwater and marine systems.

                                         6 Ocean Acidification
                                         	Changes in ocean acidification, impacting marine biodiversity and the
                                           ocean’s capacity to regulate climate.

                                         7 	Increase in Atmospheric Aerosol Loading
                                         	The rise in airborne particles from human activities or natural sources influ-
                                             ences the climate by altering temperature and precipitation patterns.

                                         8 Stratospheric Ozone Depletion
                                         	The thinning of the ozone layer in the upper atmosphere, primarily due to
                                           human-made chemicals, increases harmful UV radiation that can reach
                                           Earth’s surface.

                                         9 Introduction of Novel Entities
                                         	The introduction and accumulation of human-made substances, such as
                                           plastics and synthetic chemicals, along with the mobilization of naturally
                                           occurring elements, can disrupt important Earth system processes and pose
                                           risks of permanent damage to life in the biosphere.

                                         For more detailed information on each PB, please refer to their respective
                                         Information Sheets included later in this report (CH. 5) .

of all control variable values.

         FIGURE 6: Panel (A) presents the global mean column-averaged atmospheric CO2 concentration (XCO2) over land from 2003 to 2023, based on satellite
       observations with a spatial resolution of 5° × 5°. Panel (B) shows the spatial distribution of XCO2 across both land and ocean for the year 2023. Overall,
       the data shows a sustained increase in atmospheric CO2 concentrations between 2003 and 2023. The use of land-only XCO2 in panel (a) is motivated
       by data availability, as earlier satellite-based observations are primarily restricted to land surfaces, while combined land and ocean coverage becomes
       consistently available from 2009 onward. Additionally, the dataset is currently updated through 2023; more recent years will be incorporated once the
       corresponding processing and validation steps are completed. See reference 37 for the data and additional information.

ONE EARTH SYSTEM: WHY PLANETARY BOUNDARIES ARE CONNECTED




2.2       	One Earth System: Why Planetary
            Boundaries are Connected

The nine Planetary Boundaries are often presented level of risk associated with it. A network analy-
as separate segments of a circle. This separa- sis of the nine boundaries found that their interac-
tion is useful: it allows scientists to identify critical tions and feedbacks predominantly amplify human
Earth system processes, select measurable con- impacts, potentially narrowing the Safe Operating
trol variables and assess where risks are increas- Space compared with an assessment that treats
                                                                                       39
ing. In the Earth system itself, however, there are no every boundary independently.
walls between the boundaries. Climate, water, land,
the ocean, the atmosphere and the biosphere are
continuously connected through flows of energy, Shared drivers,
                                                      9–11
moisture, carbon, nutrients and living organisms.          different consequences
The Planetary Boundaries framework has recog-
nized this interdependence from the beginning. Planetary Boundaries are connected in two import-
Boundary levels are precautionary guardrails for ant ways. First, several boundaries share the same
individual processes, but their positions have largely underlying human drivers. Burning fossil fuels, for

  FIGURE 8: Planetary Boundaries form an interconnected causal network. The diagram shows the most significant and best-understood interactions among
Planetary Boundary (PB) processes, together with major human drivers of boundary transgression. Arrows indicate the direction of an interaction, with their
color identifying the source PB process. Arrow width represents the estimated relative strength of the interaction, while line style distinguishes reinforcing,
dampening, and mixed or context-dependent effects. Numbered drivers can affect several PB processes simultaneously. The network is selective rather
than exhaustive and reflects current scientific understanding.

KEY TAKEAWAY: Planetary Boundaries can be assessed separately, but they do not function independently. Interactions can amplify risks across the Earth
system, while shared drivers can push several boundaries at once. Understanding these connections is therefore essential for identifying actions that
create benefits across multiple boundaries without shifting pressure from one part of the Earth system to another.

   FIGURE 11: Stylized stability landscape of the Earth system. Tipping points and Planetary Boundaries are interconnected – both address Earth system
stability, but they serve distinct roles. Beyond a tipping point, a system fundamentally changes due to a shift in feedback dynamics: When stabilizing
feedbacks are dominated by destabilizing ones, they drive the system to a qualitatively different state, often abruptly and/or irreversibly. Where quan-
tifiable, the Planetary Boundaries are set in a safe distance away from risks of crossing tipping points.

                                                                                                                   SOURCE
                                  FIGURE 12: The global carbon budget and the fragile land sink. Panel A shows annual                           SINK
                                                                                                                      anthropogenic CO2 emissions  and their partitioning between atmo-
                               spheric growth, ocean uptake and land uptake, based on the Global Carbon Budget 2025.85 Positive values indicate emissions from fossil fuels and land-
                               use change, while negative values show the fraction of emissions taken up by land and ocean sinks. Panel B translates this accounting into a simplified
                               balance: Anthropogenic sources are partly offset by ocean uptake and land uptake excluding direct land-use change, with the remainder accumulating in
                               the atmosphere; the budget imbalance is shown as a residual accounting term, not as a physical sink. Panel C zooms into the green land-sink term. This
                               inset is conceptual and not to scale. It shows that land uptake excluding direct land-use change is not one process, but the residual of competing gains,
                               limits, and losses. Gains such as CO2 fertilization, longer growing seasons, forest regrowth, improved management, and soil carbon storage strengthen
                               uptake. At the same time, drought and heat stress, soil and permafrost carbon loss, biodiversity loss, habitat degradation, loss of connectivity, and hu-
                               man appropriation of plant productivity weaken sink capacity and recovery. The key message is that the land sink is still tilted toward uptake, but only
                               slightly: The global carbon buffer depends on ecological processes that are increasingly under stress.

  FIGURE 13: Spatial patterns of forest carbon stocks and net forest carbon fluxes. The left-hand map shows ecosystem carbon stocks in vegetation and
upper soil layers, highlighting regions where large carbon stores are concentrated and where degradation or disturbance could turn existing carbon
assets into future liabilities. The right-hand map shows net forest carbon fluxes from Global Forest Watch / Harris et al.125 It represents the balance
between forest carbon removals from growth and regrowth and forest carbon emissions from deforestation and stand-replacing disturbances. This is
not a complete map of the global terrestrial land carbon sink: Full land carbon fluxes could look different when non-forest ecosystems, soils, peatlands,
permafrost, lateral fluxes, and ecosystem respiration are included. Nevertheless, it gives a sharp and intuitive picture of where living forests are currently
acting as carbon sinks or sources, illustrating that the forest component of the land sink is spatially uneven and linked to land use, ecosystem integrity,
disturbance, and climate stress.

   FIGURE 14: Human pressures on the global seafloor and the water column. Six global maps illustrating the spatial extent of human activities and indus-
trial interests in the marine environment, from the coastal shelf to the abyssal plains. (A) Global bathymetry and pelagic depth zones, highlighting the
principal vertical domains of the water column, together with major basins, mid-ocean ridges, and oceanic trenches.141 (B) Non-hydrocarbon marine
infrastructure including submarine telecommunications cables (blue lines),142 major commercial ports (blue dots),143 offshore wind farms and their
associated vessel activity (green density),144 together with major sea-crossing bridges and submerged tunnels.145 Offshore wind farms remain largely
confined to the epipelagic shelf (typically <60 m for fixed foundations, extending to several hundred meters for floating systems). Combined with ship-
ping lanes and coastal engineering, this network of non-extractive built infrastructure spans the full water column from the shoreline to the deep ocean
floor (0 to >6,000 m). (C) Bottom-contacting fishing effort: trawlers and dredges. Apparent fishing effort (hours) by industrial bottom trawlers and dred-
gers, 2020–2025, based on vessel tracks derived from Automatic Identification Systems (AIS).146 Bottom trawling currently impacts the seabed from
the coastline to roughly 4,000 m depth, and the operating depth has been steadily increasing as continental shelves are depleted and gear is adapted
for deeper grounds. The footprint highlights chronic seabed disturbance along continental shelves, slopes, and seamounts. (D) Global shipping densi-
ty. Cumulative density of commercial vessel traffic, revealing the principal trans-oceanic shipping corridors and choke points.146–148 Although vessels
themselves operate at the surface (epipelagic), shipping lanes are sustained by a coastal-to-deep-sea infrastructure footprint (ports, dredged channels,
anchorages, cables, and pipelines) extending from 0 to more than 6,000 m. (E) Polymetallic nodule fields and exploration contracts. Composite map of
abyssal areas geologically favorable to manganese-nodule formation (beige areas);149 documented nodule occurrences enriched in iron (blue dots) and
manganese (red dots);150 and polymetallic-nodule exploration contract areas issued by the International Seabed Authority in the high seas (black poly-
gons).151 Whereas seabed mining as a whole could potentially affect any depth from the shore to 6,000 m, commercial interest in polymetallic nodules is
concentrated in the abyssopelagic zone, between approximately 4,000 and 6,000 m. (F) Offshore hydrocarbon system: extraction, transport, and export.
End-to-end footprint of the marine hydrocarbon value chain, from wellhead to liquefaction terminal: offshore oil-and-gas platforms and their associated
vessel activity (brown density, 2017–2021);144 submarine oil and gas pipelines (red lines);152 LNG export and import terminals (red dots) and the global
density of LNG tanker traffic linking them.153 The hydrocarbon system reaches from coastal LNG terminals through the epipelagic, mesopelagic, and
bathypelagic zones into the abyssopelagic, with platforms now operating at depths exceeding 3,000 m.

  FIGURE 15: In bottom-contact fishing, weighted nets are dragged across the seafloor at speed, destroying bottom-dwelling life indiscriminately
and swirling up great amounts of sediment. Top: Photographed from inside a trawl net, which is churning up the sediments as it is being dragged
across the seafloor. Middle left: Intact seabed off the island of Arran, Scotland, with juvenile cod swimming over a healthy seagrass meadow. Midd-
le right: The same area, recently devastated by scallop dredge fishing. Bottom: Aerial view of churned up sediments from bottom-contact fishing.

    FIGURE 16: Novel Entities pose significant threats to the functioning of the planet, as well as for human well-being. Much is already known about wide-
 spread contamination with various substances, and observations of additional destructive impacts are emerging. However, some of the greatest risks
 lie in the “unknowns”: impacts of pollution that we are not yet aware of, but which may already be causing grave harm, or may do so in the future. By the
 time these effects become widely recognized and understood, it may be too late to reverse them.158,160,161,172,182,186–193

5.1        	Climate
             Change

             Climate Change is disrupting the Earth’s climate system in ways that threaten the founda-
             tions of human well being. By burning fossil fuels, clearing forests, and altering the land,
             humanity is releasing large amounts of greenhouse gases that trap extra heat in the atmo-
TAKEAWAY




             sphere. This is pushing the planet beyond its natural limits. As a result, global temperatures
             are increasing, ice sheets are melting, and weather patterns are becoming more extreme.
             Sea levels are rising, and ecosystems are under stress.

             These changes increase the risk of crossing dangerous tipping points – irreversible shifts
             in Earth’s systems that could lock in long-term damage. Communities around the world are
MAIN




             already experiencing more heatwaves, droughts, floods, and storms. Without urgent action,
             continued climate disruption risks triggering irreversible changes in Earth’s systems, under-
             mining ecosystems, human health, and societal stability for generations to come.




               CO₂                         PB            High-Risk Line                                    Atmospheric CO₂ concentration [ppm]
               CONCENTRATION             350 ppm            450 ppm

                                                        426 ppm

               FORCING                   +1 W/m2           +1.5 W/m2                                                at top-of-atmosphere [W/m2]

             Definition                                                        2026 Status

             Climate Change refers to long-term shifts in tem-                 The atmospheric concentration of CO2 (426 ppm)
             perature and weather patterns. The Planetary                      and the total anthropogenic radiative forcing at
             Boundary for Climate Change is defined to avoid                   the top of the atmosphere (+ 3.10 W/m²) have long
             transgressing levels of climate-system change                     exceeded their safe levels (350 ppm and 1 W/ m²,
             beyond which the risks of triggering irreversible                 respectively). This transgression is dangerously
             changes, such as large-scale melting of polar ice                 increasing global temperatures, intensifying climate
             sheets, increase significantly. A necessary condition             impacts, and increasing the likelihood of crossing
             for the overall stability of the climate system is to             critical tipping points in Earth’s climate system.
             limit the accumulation of heat within the Earth sys-
             tem. This accumulation results from persistent per-
             turbations to Earth’s energy balance, i.e., the bal-
             ance between incoming solar radiation and outgoing
             energy. Today, human activities are the dominant
             cause of these perturbations, most notably through
             the emission of greenhouse gases, such as CO2,
             which reduce the loss of heat to space and thereby
             destabilize the climate system.

CLIMATE CHANGE




            CONTROL VARIABLE


            Atmospheric CO2 Concentration (CO2)



            DEFINITION                       Atmospheric CO2 is a primary greenhouse gas emitted by human activities such
                                             as fossil fuel combustion, deforestation, and cement production. It is a key driver
                                                                 11
                                             of Climate Change. CO2 is relatively straightforward to monitor through atmo-
                                             spheric measurements, satellite observations, and carbon budget assessments.

            UNIT		                           Parts per million (ppm)

            HISTORICAL                       Over the course of Earth’s history, CO2 levels have naturally fluctuated between
            RANGE                            about 180–200 ppm during ice ages and around 280 ppm during the pre-in-
                                                                       194
                                             dustrial Holocene period.

            PLANETARY                        Scientists have proposed a PB for CO2 at 350 ppm, based on paleoclimate evi-
            BOUNDARY                         dence and climate modeling. This threshold represents a point beyond which
            (PB)                             the risks of triggering irreversible changes, such as large-scale melting of polar
                                                                                  71,195–197
                                             ice sheets, increase significantly.             It also aligns with the internationally
                                             recognized goal of limiting global warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial lev-
                                                                                                         10
                                             els, as agreed upon in the Paris Climate Agreement.

               FIGURE 17: Atmospheric CO2 concentration far exceeds the safe level. This figure illustrates the steady increase in the
            atmospheric CO2 concentration, as one of the Climate Change boundary’s control variables. The blue line represents
            annual mean values from 1959 to 2025 at the Mauna Loa Observatory in Hawaii, operated by the National Oceanic and
            Atmospheric Administration (NOAA).198 The dark violet line shows globally averaged CO2 concentrations from mul-
            tiple international monitoring sites,198 including Mauna Loa. The green and red lines indicate the baseline (safe) value
            (280 ppm) and the Planetary Boundary threshold (350 ppm) for this control variable, respectively.

            KEY TAKEAWAY: CO2 continues to rise and remains the dominant driver of climate change, with current levels approxi-
            mately 50% above pre-industrial concentrations.

             CONTROL VARIABLE


             Total Anthropogenic Radiative Forcing at the
             Top of the Atmosphere (TOA)

             DEFINITION                                                               The net radiative forcing sums up all the ways human activities impact the
                                                                                      global climate. This includes emissions of greenhouse gases such as carbon
                                                                                      dioxide (CO2), methane (CH4), and nitrous oxide (N2O), as well as aerosols and
                                                                                      land-use changes. Radiative forcing is a key measure of how much additional
                                                                                      heat energy is added to the Earth system. It integrates the effects of all human
                                                                                      activities that influence the planet’s energy balance and therefore represents
                                                                                                                                              11
                                                                                      the overall strength of human-induced climate change. While changes in TOA
                                                                                      fluxes can be measured via satellites, estimating the human-caused compo-
                                                                                      nent depends on climate models. This makes it a powerful, system-level signal,
                                                                                      but also more abstract and less directly observable than CO2 or temperature.

             UNIT 		                                                                  Watts per square meter (W/m²)

             HISTORICAL                                                               During the pre-industrial Holocene, the radiative forcing remained relatively sta-
                                                                                                                                                                   199,200
             RANGE                                                                    ble with small fluctuations due to land cover changes and volcanic activity,
                                                                                      indicating a stable energy balance under which human civilizations developed.
                                                                                      Significant positive forcing began with the Industrial Revolution, as greenhouse
                                                                                                                                                                      201
                                                                                      gas concentrations rose due to fossil fuel combustion and land conversion.

             PLANETARY                                                                Scientists have proposed a PB for total anthropogenic radiative forcing at +1.0
             BOUNDARY                                                                 W/m², relative to pre-industrial levels. This threshold is based on the climate
             (PB)                                                                     system’s sensitivity to greenhouse gas forcing, observed responses of polar ice
                                                                                      sheets to warming, and growing evidence of climate instability at forcing levels
                                                                                                         9
                                                                                      above +1.5 W/m². Exceeding this boundary increases the risk of irreversible
                                                                                      climate impacts and triggering long-term destabilizing system feedback loops.



                                                                    3.0




                                                                    2.5

                FIGURE 18: Disturbance of our planet’s energy balance. This figure shows the global average of human-induced radia-
             tive forcing at the top of the atmosphere from 1750 to 2025.21,202 The values were calculated using observational data
             and established climate models, based on methods from the IPCC. The green line marks the safe baseline (0.0 W/m²),
             and the red line indicates the Planetary Boundary (+1.0 W/m²).

             KEY TAKEAWAY: Human activities have significantly increased net radiative forcing to three times the safe limit, exerting
             a persistent warming influence on the Earth system and pushing well beyond the safe threshold.

5.2        	Change in
             Biosphere Integrity

             Biosphere Integrity refers to the collective capacity of ecosystems across the planet to sup-
             port life and maintain the overall health and stability of the Earth system. This depends on the
             composition and diversity of species, and their interactions as ecosystems. Human activities
             are putting increasing pressure on Biosphere Integrity through land-use change such as the
TAKEAWAY




             expansion and intensification of agriculture and livestock production, as well as urban and
             industrial development, overexploitation of natural resources (like overfishing), the spread
             of invasive species, and various forms of pollution.

             These pressures are further intensified by disruptions in other Planetary Boundaries, includ-
             ing Climate Change, Modification of Biogeochemical Flows (e.g. fertilizer pollution), and
MAIN




             Novel Entities (e.g. chemical pollution by toxins and plastics). Today, the Planetary Boundary
             for Biosphere Integrity is strongly transgressed. When Biosphere Integrity is compromised,
             Earth system resilience is weakened, threatening the essential conditions that support human
             life on our planet.

PLANETARY BOUNDARY INFORMATION SHEETS




                   CONTROL VARIABLE


                   Genetic Diversity: Species Extinction Rate



                   DEFINITION                                           The decline in genetic diversity, along with the loss of their habitats, threat-
                                                                        ens the biosphere’s ability to co-regulate the state of the planet by impact-
                                                                        ing the energy balance and chemical cycles on Earth. The Species Extinction
                                                                        Rate describes the speed at which the number of species (species richness) is
                                                                        reduced. High species extinction rates negatively impact ecosystem resilience
                                                                        because fewer species can lead to changes in ecological processes and spe-
                                                                        cies interactions that maintain ecosystem stability. Species extinction rates are
                                                                        used provisionally as a proxy for loss in genetic diversity and may be refined
                                                                        as more data becomes available.

                   UNIT                                                 Extinctions per Million Species-Years (E/MSY). For example, if 1 species out of
                                                                        1 million species goes extinct every year, the extinction rate would be 1 E/MSY.

                   HISTORICAL                                           The background (i.e. normal) rate of extinction loss is estimated to be 1 E/
                                                                            228
                   RANGE                                                MSY.

                                                                                                    11
                   PLANETARY                                            The PB is set at 10 E/MSY because, according to latest expert advice, los-
                   BOUNDARY                                             ing species at a rate 10x faster than the natural background rate is what the
                   (PB)                                                 Earth system can tolerate. The boundary has a wide range of uncertainty (10–
                                                                        100 E/MSY), with the safe threshold reflecting uncertainty and precaution to
                                                                        account for potential knowledge gaps and to minimize the risk of significant
                                                                        Earth system changes.

             CONTROL VARIABLE


             Functional Integrity: Human Appropriation
             of Net Primary Production (HANPP)

             DEFINITION              Net primary production (NPP) is the rate at which plants and other photosyn-
                                     thetic organisms produce biomass (organic carbon) in an ecosystem, after
                                     accounting for the carbon they use for their own respiration (gross primary
                                     production minus respiration). NPP is a fundamental measure of ecosystem
                                     productivity and health. It serves as a proxy for the energy flow into the bio-
                                     sphere that all life processes depend on. Human appropriated NPP (HANPP)
                                     measures the extent to which human activities, such as agriculture, forestry,
                                     and urbanization, appropriate NPP for human use by (1) inhibiting net primary
                                     production and (2) withdrawing energy by harvesting products for human use
                                     and consumption.

             UNIT                    HANPP is measured in petagrams of carbon appropriated per year. In the con-
                                     text of Planetary Boundaries, HANPP is reported as a fraction of the natural
                                     Holocene reference NPP (i.e. energy flows into the biosphere without humans).

             HISTORICAL              Without any human activity, HANPP would be zero. For low-impact early human
             RANGE                   societies, it would be close to zero.

             PLANETARY               The PB is provisionally set at less than 10% of the Holocene reference NPP, and
             BOUNDARY                shifts into the zone of high risk at 20%. These thresholds are based on obser-
             (PB)                    vational data and on ecological models that indicate negative trends in sev-
                                                                                      10,106
                                     eral critical metrics of biosphere functioning.         As NPP is the basis for the
                                                                                                             233
                                     energy and materials flow that underpins the biosphere’s functioning, today’s
                                     impact of HANPP reflects the significant declines seen across major indicators
                                     of ecosystem health.

                                      Kastner et al. 2022

             (2013) with a Holocene-like potential NPP derived from LPJmL and CLIMBER-X simulations. The lower HANPP values

PLANETARY BOUNDARY INFORMATION SHEETS




5.3        	Land System
             Change

              Forests play a vital role in stabilizing the Earth’s climate, supporting biodiversity, and sus-
              taining human well-being. Ongoing forest loss and other land system changes continue at
              alarming rates, driven by complex and region-specific pressures.

              Agriculture is the leading direct cause of Land System Change, particularly livestock grazing
              and the expansion of cropland. Other significant factors include illegal logging, infrastruc-
TAKEAWAY




              ture expansion, weak governance, and climate change. Often, these points are interrelated
              and facilitate each other. In the Amazon, for example, deforestation typically starts with the
              extraction of illegal timber before land is finally converted into pasture.

              The impact of deforestation is profound. It disrupts biodiversity, increases carbon emissions,
              alters rainfall patterns, and weakens soil and water systems. Deforestation also affects human
MAIN




              health, for example by increasing the risk of malaria in affected areas. Climate Change and
              water stress further amplify this damage through heatwaves, fires, and manifestation of pests
              such as outbreaks of bark beetle. If land degradation continues, our chances of stabilizing the
              climate, securing freshwater resources, and protecting biodiversity will be weakened. To stay
              within the Safe Operating Space, we must urgently protect and restore ecosystems, keep-
              ing the land resilient, and ensuring that the Earth remains habitable for generations to come.

                       CONTROL VARIABLE


                       Forest Area


                       DEFINITION                           While different land types have distinct functions within the Earth system, for-
                                                            ests are of particular importance to the climate system, due to their tempera-
                                                            ture- and moisture-regulating interactions with the atmosphere and their role
                                                                                         258
                                                            in carbon and water cycling. ’Remaining forest area’ is the main component
                                                            responsible for maintaining major global ecological functions, and is thus used
                                                            as the control variable for this Planetary Boundary. It is expressed as a per-
                                                            centage of the forest cover that would exist in the absence of human land-use
                                                            changes (‘potential natural forest cover‘). This is determined for the boreal,
                                                            temperate, and tropical forest biomes (as contiguous area per continent) and
                                                            then aggregated globally.

                       UNIT                                 Percentage (%) of potential forest cover.

                       HISTORICAL                           The value of relative forest cover ranges from 100% (all forest relative to poten-
                       RANGE                                tial forest cover is remaining) to 0% (no forest relative to potential forest cover
                                                            is remaining).

                       PLANETARY                            The global Planetary Boundary threshold for Land System Change is set at 78%
                       BOUNDARY                             of the potential forest cover. Changed from the previous 75%, it is now cal-
                       (PB)                                 culated as the area-weighted mean of the contiguous biome thresholds: 85%
                                                                                                              2
                                                            for boreal and tropical forests (both 19.1 Mkm ), and 50% for temperate for-
                                                                           2 11
                                                            ests (9.9 Mkm ). These thresholds are based on studies indicating that trop-
                                                            ical and boreal forests exert a stronger influence on regional and remote cli-
                                                                                                      258,259
                                                            matic conditions than temperate forests.          The areas differ from the total
                                                                    260  258
                                                            areas in or because only contiguous forest cover is taken into account in
                                                            the PB framework.

   FIGURE 29: Global risk map of the Land System Change boundary transgression – Forest area. Transgression is shown for the major contiguous forest
biomes as defined in Steffen et al. 2015.11 Transgression is based on the control variable of remaining forest area. Lighter shades of a color indicate areas
that were originally covered with forest but are now predominantly deforested. Areas without contiguous potential forest cover are shown in gray. This
is based on data from Brun et al. 2022, Copernicus (2024) and Ramankutty & Foley (1999).260–262

KEY TAKEAWAY : Most large contiguous forest biomes of the Earth have transgressed the Planetary Boundary, although with varying degrees of trans-
gression. Following the inclusion of two previously missing forest cover classes from the Copernicus dataset in our analysis, the tropical and temperate
biomes of the Americas now have a slightly better status compared to previous assessments. However, this does not mean that these biomes are in a
good condition. Our methodology does not capture forest degradation resulting from deforestation, extraction of resources or destruction from extreme
weather events, which are amplified by climate change.263 This means that we should not feel too confident about the apparent good state in the Ama-
zon, since this system is likely still at risk.264

PLANETARY BOUNDARY INFORMATION SHEETS




5.4          	Freshwater
               Change

               Human actions change how freshwater flows and stocks are distributed on land and in the
               atmosphere. Specifically greenhouse gas emissions, irrigation and other water uses, water
               infrastructures, and the expansion of agricultural land have changed the global water cycle.

               This has pervasive impacts throughout the water cycle; the occurrence of droughts and
TAKEAWAY




               extreme precipitation has increased, while groundwater stocks are often declining and river
               flows to the ocean are widely interrupted. The Planetary Boundary for Freshwater Change
               measures how far streamflow (blue water) and soil moisture (green water) deviate from a
               quasi-stable state that is largely unaffected by human actions.

               Anomalously dry or wet streamflow and soil moisture conditions now occur on approximately
MAIN




               twice as much land as they did under pre-industrial-like conditions, due to direct and indi-
               rect human pressures on the water cycle. This surpasses the safe limit for human modifica-
               tion of the water cycle and places the Freshwater Change boundary in the increasing risk
               zone. Increasing freshwater anomalies may undermine terrestrial and aquatic ecosystem
               integrity, modify land carbon balance and affect hydro-climatic processes. They also change
               how water is available for human uses, which may compromise benefits that are gained by
               managing freshwater resources.

                  CONTROL VARIABLE


                  Blue Water


                  DEFINITION                                                   Human-induced disturbance of blue water, which refers to water in rivers, lakes,
                                                                               reservoirs, groundwater and wetlands, is approximated by the annual global
                                                                               area with significant deviations in streamflow from variability under the baseline
                                                                               (pre-industrial-like) state. The boundary reflects changes in blue water avail-
                                                                               ability, which are crucial for the health of associated aquatic ecosystems and
                                                                               for water supply to human societies.

                  UNIT                                                         Percentage (%) of annual global ice-free land area.

                  HISTORICAL                                                   The percentage of land area experiencing significant deviations in streamflow
                  RANGE                                                        can range from 0% (no area affected) to 100% (all ice-free land area affected),
                                                                               with a baseline median value of about 10.3% representing natural variabil-
                                                                               ity. The baseline conditions approximate an Earth system state largely unaf-
                                                                               fected by humans, based on a 119-year long pre-industrial-like scenario with
                                                                               no anthropogenic climate change but with human forcing (land and water use)
                                                                               at year 1901 levels.

                  PLANETARY                                                    The PB is set at 12.9% of the global ice-free land area experiencing significant
                  BOUNDARY                                                     dry or wet deviations in streamflow. This corresponds to the 95th percentile of
                  (PB)                                                         the baseline state variability, during which anomalously dry or wet local con-
                                                                               ditions occurred annually with a likelihood of less than 5% on at least 12.9% of
                                                                               the global area.

5.5        	Modification of
              Biogeochemical Flows

                          th
             In the 20 century, the invention of industrial nitrogen fixation made it possible to convert
             molecular nitrogen from the atmosphere into reactive forms, such as those used in inor-
             ganic fertilizers. Combined with the mining of phosphate rock, this led to a drastic increase
TAKEAWAY




             in fertilizer application on agricultural land. Large quantities of both nutrients either remain
             directly on pastures and fields or enter the environment through animal manure and sewage.

             Phosphorus attaches to soil and sediment, while nitrogen is released into the air and stored
             in groundwater and surface waters. Both nutrients are released into surface waters via soil
             erosion, surface runoff, and wastewater discharge. Excess nutrients can negatively affect
MAIN




             biodiversity and the resilience of ecosystems on land, in freshwater, and in the ocean, envi-
             ronments which are adapted to specific nutrient levels. Currently, losses of both nutrients
             to the environment are disrupting ecosystems beyond the safe level.




               PHOSPHORUS                  PB            High-Risk Line                                              Application of mined P
                                        6.2 Tg/yr          11.2 Tg/yr                                                    to cropland [Tg/yr]



                                                                          2026 Assessment
                                                                             18.2 Tg/yr




               NITROGEN                    PB           High-Risk Line                                                   Intentional N fixation
                                        62 Tg/yr           82 Tg/yr                                                      for agriculture [Tg/yr]



                                                                                                 2026 Assessment
                                                                                                    165 Tg/yr




             Definition                                                       2026 Status

             Biogeochemical flows are the movement of the two                 Both the application of mined phosphorus to crop-
                                                                                                     322
             key elements, nitrogen and phosphorus, through                   land (18.2 Tg P/year) and the anthropogenic fix-
             the environment and organisms. Both nitrogen and                 ation of nitrogen for agriculture (extracting nitrogen
                                                                                                                       52,323
             phosphorus are required for fundamental biological               from the atmosphere; 165 Tg N/year)             are dis-
             functions, and their availability in soils, freshwater           rupting the corresponding nutrient cycles beyond
             and the ocean were once a major limiting factor for              the safe levels of 6.2 Tg P/year and 62 Tg N/year,
                                               321
             biological productivity on Earth. Human activities               respectively.
             are massively changing the magnitude of these nutri-
             ent cycles, with potentially severe consequences
             for the Earth system. The Planetary Boundary for
             Modification of Biogeochemical Flows is defined
             to keep these human changes at a safe level.

PLANETARY BOUNDARY INFORMATION SHEETS




                     CONTROL VARIABLE


                    Phosphorus (P) Flows


                     DEFINITION                                The phosphorus boundary consists of a regional component, aiming to prevent
                                                               eutrophication of freshwater systems, and a global component, aiming to pre-
                                                               vent large-scale ocean anoxia over geological time scales. The regional bound-
                                                               ary uses the application of mined phosphorus to erodible soils as an indica-
                                                               tor of phosphorus flow into freshwater systems, while the global boundary is
                                                               based on riverine transport of phosphorus to the ocean.

                     UNIT                                      Teragrams of Phosphorus per Year (Tg of P/year). 1 teragram equals 1 million
                                                               metric tons.

                     HISTORICAL                                Before human intervention, phosphorus flows were low (2.5 Tg P/year from
                                                                                                                            324
                     RANGE                                     land to freshwater and 1.3 Tg P/year of export to the ocean). Human activ-
                                                               ities have increased flows from land to freshwater systems through a global
                                                               application of mined phosphorus to cropland to around 18.2 Tg P/year (regional,
                                                               aggregated) and have increased phosphorus flows to the ocean to around 4.4
                                                                                                                  322,324
                                                               Tg P/year (global), largely due to fertilizer use.

                                                                                                                                        11
                     PLANETARY                                 The regional boundary is set to an application of 6.2 Tg P per year, while the
                     BOUNDARY                                  global boundary is established at a flow of 11 Tg P per year, which is roughly
                                                                                               9
                    (PB)                                       ten times the natural flow rate. Here, we focus on the regional boundary, as it
                                                               sets a stricter threshold than the ocean boundary.

                    (2024).322

                    KEY TAKEAWAY: The significant increase over time in phosphorus application highlights the growing reliance on phos-
                    phorus in agriculture over the decades.

                   CONTROL VARIABLE


                  Nitrogen (N) Fixation


                   DEFINITION                            The nitrogen boundary sets a threshold for anthropogenic nitrogen fixation in
                                                         agriculture. It is the sum of industrial nitrogen fixation through the Haber-Bosch
                                                         process for fertilizer production and the intentional biological fixation through
                                                         leguminous plants that host nitrogen-fixing bacteria in their roots.

                   UNIT                                  Teragrams of Nitrogen per Year (Tg of N/year). 1 teragram equals 1 million met-
                                                         ric tons.

                   HISTORICAL                            Human activities have increased anthropogenic nitrogen fixation rates from 0
                   RANGE                                 to approximately 190 Tg of N/year globally, mainly through fertilizer use. As a
                                                         result, human interference in the global nitrogen cycle now exceeds the total
                                                                                                                                        325
                                                         flux of fixed nitrogen from all natural sources, both on land and in the ocean.

                   PLANETARY                             The nitrogen PB targets industrial and intentional biological nitrogen fixation,
                   BOUNDARY                              setting a threshold at 62 Tg N/year. This boundary is established based on
                  (PB)                                   empirical data, environmental considerations, and the precautionary principle
                                                                                                                               11
                                                         to uphold biogeochemical balance and safeguard ecosystem health.

PBSCIENCE | PLANETARY HEALTH CHECK 2026                                                                                                   83
MODIFICATION OF BIOGEOCHEMICAL FLOWS




5.6        	Ocean
              Acidification

              The ocean absorbs around 25% of the CO2 released by human activities. This oceanic uptake
              slows climate change but causes the seawater to become more acidic – a process known as
              ocean acidification. Ocean acidification has now gone beyond what is considered safe for
              marine life. A key indicator of ocean acidification is the aragonite saturation state. Aragonite
TAKEAWAY




              is a form of calcium carbonate that many marine organisms – like corals and shellfish – use
              to build their shells and skeletons.

              As more CO2 enters the ocean, it forms carbonic acid, which lowers the pH but also reduces
              the availability of carbonate ions. Increased acidity and reduced carbonate ion availability
              can make it more energetically costly for calcifying organisms to build and maintain their
MAIN




              shells or skeletons. Also many non-calcifying organisms experience adverse effects on their
              physiology, growth and survival due to increasing acidity. In many regions, vulnerable spe-
              cies are already exposed to harmful conditions. Cold-water corals, tropical corals, Arctic
              calcifiers, and marine life in coastal upwelling systems are especially at risk as acidification
              continues to spread and intensify.

                       2.86 Ω arag       2.50 Ω arag (provisional)                                of aragonite in surface seawater [1]

                  CONTROL VARIABLE


                  Aragonite Saturation State (Ω)


                  DEFINITION                                 Aragonite is a form of calcium carbonate that many marine organisms – such
                                                             as corals and shellfish – use to build their shells and skeletons. The aragonite
                                                             saturation state (Ω) reflects the availability of carbonate ions in seawater rel-
                                                             ative to the amount needed for saturation with aragonite, with values below
                                                             1 indicating corrosive conditions. Ω is closely tied to CO2 levels in the atmo-
                                                             sphere, making it a useful indicator of the impact of rising CO2 on both ocean
                                                             chemistry and marine organisms.

                  UNIT                                       Dimensionless.

                  HISTORICAL                                 Ω varies by region, predominantly ranging from 3.3 to 4 in tropical regions to
                  RANGE                                      1 to 2 in polar regions.

                  PLANETARY                                  The PB is set at the global mean surface Ω value of 2.86, which is 80% of the
                  BOUNDARY                                   pre-industrial value of 3.57. The 80% threshold was selected with the aim of
                  (PB)                                       preventing large-scale aragonite undersaturation in high-latitude waters, while
                                                             maintaining well-oversaturated conditions in low-latitude regions, and thereby
                                                             limiting harmful impacts on marine calcifiers.

OCEAN ACIDIFICATION




5.7        	Increase in Atmospheric
              Aerosol Loading

              Atmospheric Aerosol Loading refers to the concentration of small particles suspended in
              the atmosphere, which originate from both natural processes like dust storms and volcanic
              eruptions, and human activities such as burning fossil fuels, industrial processes, and agri-
              culture. These particles influence the Earth’s climate by interacting with sunlight and clouds.
TAKEAWAY




              Some reflect sunlight and cool the planet (such as sulfates), while others absorb heat and
              contribute to warming (such as black carbon). Aerosols also affect cloud formation and pre-
              cipitation patterns, making their climate effects complex and regionally varied.

              In recent decades, efforts to improve air quality have led to declining aerosol emissions in
              many higher-income regions, while emission levels remain high or are increasing in parts of
MAIN




              Asia and Africa due to rapid industrial growth and biomass burning. Overall, global anthro-
              pogenic aerosol loading is decreasing, which helps to reduce air pollution and health risks
              but may accelerate global warming by removing the cooling effect that some aerosols pro-
              vide. Managing aerosol emissions presents a trade-off: While their reduction benefits human
              health and ecosystems, it also reveals more of the warming caused by greenhouse gases.

              CONTROL VARIABLE


              Interhemispheric Difference in Aerosol Optical Depth (AOD)


              DEFINITION                AOD measures how many aerosols (small particles suspended in the air) block
                                        the transmission of light in the atmosphere, without distinguishing whether the
                                        particles absorb or reflect light. The interhemispheric difference refers to how
                                        much higher AOD tends to be in the Northern Hemisphere – where most human
                                        activity and emissions occur – compared to the Southern Hemisphere. Globally,
                                        this control variable measures the interhemispheric difference in aerosol con-
                                        centrations between the Northern and Southern Hemispheres.

              UNIT                      Dimensionless.

              HISTORICAL                AOD values range from 0 (no aerosols) to 1 or higher (very dense aerosol layer).
              RANGE                     The difference in AOD between the Northern and Southern Hemispheres has
                                        been decreasing from 2010 to 2025 and ranging between 0.08 and 0.06.

              PLANETARY                 The PB is defined by an interhemispheric difference in AOD of 0.1. This thresh-
              BOUNDARY                  old is based on observational evidence from volcanic eruptions and on mod-
              (PB)                      eling studies, which suggest that a rising interhemispheric difference in AOD
                                        can trigger regional-scale tipping points, potentially leading to shifts in mon-
                                        soonal patterns. Such changes can significantly affect weather cycles, increas-
                                        ing the risk of floods and droughts. This threshold is also relevant in the con-
                                                                                                 55
                                        text of justice considerations related to human health.

  FIGURE 42: Global total aerosol optical depth (AOD) at 550 nm, time-averaged over 2010–2020. The Copernicus Atmosphere Monitoring Service (CAMS)
global reanalysis (EAC4) integrates satellite retrievals and model simulations to produce consistent, spatially and temporally resolved fields of atmospheric
composition. Total AOD represents the aerosol optical depth due to all aerosol species, including sulfates, black carbon, organic matter, sea salt, and dust.

KEY TAKEAWAY: High AOD values occur over major dust source regions (such as the Sahara and Arabian Peninsula), biomass-burning zones (such as Central
Africa and the Amazon), and industrially polluted areas (such as India and Eastern China). Low AOD is found over remote oceans and the polar regions.




  FIGURE 43: Effective radiative forcing (W/m²) of sulfur dioxide and black carbon, averaged annually over 2010–2020. Effective radiative forcing (ERF)
estimates for sulfur dioxide (SO2, left) and black carbon (BC, right) are shown, based on simulations from CMIP6 models participating in the Aerosols
and Chemistry Model Intercomparison Project (AerChemMIP). ERF represents the net change in Earth’s energy balance due to these aerosol emissions,
including both direct radiative effects and interactions with clouds. SO2 generally exerts a cooling effect by increasing reflective sulfate aerosols, while
BC leads to warming by absorbing sunlight in the atmosphere.

KEY TAKEAWAY: Different kinds of aerosols have both significant warming and cooling effects on the climate system.
