INCREASE IN ATMOSPHERIC AEROSOL LOADING




             CONTROL VARIABLE


             Proposed regional control variable:
             Aerosol Optical Depth over South Asia

             DEFINITION          No globally uniform AOD threshold can currently be defined for climate impacts
                                 because the interactions of aerosol loading with climate effects vary strongly
                                 across regions. Instead, the global control variable is the interhemispheric aero-
                                 sol optical depth difference. A regional boundary has, however, been identified
                                 for South Asia, where aerosol loading affects the South Asian summer mon-
                                 soon, a key component of the global hydrological cycle. The regional aerosol
                                 boundary for South Asia is defined using AOD as the control variable, repre-
                                 senting the column aerosol loading over the South Asian region. Regional AOD
                                 correlates with PM2.5 (fine particulate matter with diameters 2.5 micrometers,
                                 a health-hazardous air pollutant, which is also particularly important in the con-
                                                                                             55,392                  393
                                 text of justice considerations regarding human health.             ) concentration.
                                 However, this regional correlation is not yet fully integrated into the Planetary
                                 Boundaries framework.

             UNIT                Dimensionless.

             HISTORICAL          AOD values range from 0 (no aerosols) to 1 or higher (very dense aerosol layer);
             RANGE               0.32 at present over South Asia.

                                           11
             PLANETARY           Following , a safe boundary of AOD 0.25 (Range 0.25–0.5) is proposed, with a
             BOUNDARY            zone of uncertainty extending to AOD 0.5, beyond which substantial disruption
             (PB)                to the regional hydrological cycle is expected. This boundary has been recently
                                                                              394
                                 confirmed using climate model simulations, which show that increasing AOD
                                 to 0.25 leads to a 10% reduction in South Asian monsoon precipitation (drought
                                 conditions), while AOD 0.5 results in 15–20% reductions in the rainfall, indicat-
                                 ing severe monsoon weakening.

5.8        	Stratospheric
              Ozone Depletion


              The ozone layer in the upper atmosphere protects the Earth from harmful solar ultravio-
TAKEAWAY




              let radiation. Additionally, ozone plays an important role in atmospheric energy balance.
              Because of the release of ozone depleting substances (ODS), the ozone layer has substan-
                                                            th
              tially declined over the second half of the 20 century.

              Since the mid-1990s, thanks to the international agreements limiting ODS release, the ozone
              layer has largely recovered, and it is expected to stay within the safe limits. The ozone layer
MAIN




              depletion and its subsequent recovery had measurable effects on atmospheric circulation,
              particularly in the Southern Hemisphere.

             CONTROL VARIABLE


             Stratospheric Ozone Concentration


             DEFINITION                         Average stratospheric ozone concentration in the extra-polar zone – the
                                                region of the Earth’s atmosphere outside the polar areas, spanning from 60°N
                                                to 60°S. While the polar ‘ozone hole’ is widely known, the impacts on humans
                                                and ecosystems are more severe when it comes to the extra-polar ozone layer.
                                                Additionally, the ozone hole phenomenon involves complex factors beyond just
                                                anthropogenic ozone-depleting substances, making extra-polar ozone a more
                                                                                             9
                                                relevant measure for a Planetary Boundary.

             UNIT                               Dobson Unit (DU). One DU represents a layer of ozone that would be 0.01 mil-
                                                limeters thick under standard temperature and pressure.

             HISTORICAL                         Typical local values range from about 100 to 500 DU.
             RANGE


             PLANETARY                          There is no clear threshold for defining a boundary for global extra-polar strato-
             BOUNDARY                           spheric ozone. As a preliminary estimate, the PB is set as a maximum reduction
             (PB)                               of 5% from the reference level (mean of the years 1964–1980). With the ref-
                                                erence level estimated at 292 DU, the Planetary Boundary is set at 277.4 DU.

5.9        	Introduction of
             Novel Entities

              Novel entities comprise human-made chemicals and materials and naturally occurring sub-
              stances that are mobilized by humans. Produced and released in myriad forms and huge
              quantities, they can interact, accumulate and elicit impacts in ways that can threaten the Earth
              system. For example, many novel entities (certain pollutants and plastics) are widespread
              globally with poor reversibility. They accumulate along the food chain and cause adverse
TAKEAWAY




              effects, by themselves or in combination with other stressors (e.g., heat or drought), ulti-
              mately with the potential to threaten biosphere integrity.

              However, our understanding of planetary-scale exposure and impacts is still fragmented due
              to the tremendous, ever-increasing number and diversity of novel entities. To assess the
              potential planetary risk arising from our ignorance, we use a practical proxy: the proportion of
MAIN




              novel entities that does not undergo adequate safety testing before widespread production
              and use. That share is currently very high, and on this basis the Planetary Boundary for Novel
              Entities is considered transgressed – the creation of novel entities far outpaces our capacity
              to evaluate them. Future assessments may incorporate additional metrics, such as produc-
              tion, release to the environment, transport to and accumulation in remote sinks, and impacts.




                          PB           High-Risk Line                                     Fraction of synthetic chemicals released
                          0%                                                                   without adequate safety testing [%]

PLANETARY BOUNDARY INFORMATION SHEETS




             CONTROL VARIABLE


             Percentage of novel entities released to the environment
             without adequate safety testing

             DEFINITION                     The share of human-made or -mobilized chemicals and materials released into
                                            or mobilized in the environment without adequate safety testing. This control
                                            variable reflects our level of understanding of the risks of novel entities and
                                            how effective regulations are. Monitoring this share is essential for managing
                                            the potential impacts these substances may have on the Earth system.

             UNIT                           Percentage, i.e. the fraction of synthetic chemicals and materials released with-
                                            out adequate safety testing.

             HISTORICAL                     This percentage can vary from 0% (all chemicals and materials undergo suffi-
             RANGE                          cient testing before release) to 100% (none are tested).

             PLANETARY                      Following the precautionary principle, the Planetary Boundary of Novel Entities
             BOUNDARY                       is set at 0 % – no human-made or -mobilized chemicals and materials should
                                                                                                         10
             (PB)                           be released or mobilized without adequate safety testing. Examples of novel
                                            entities include polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs), which, even decades after
                                            their ban in the Stockholm Convention, are still present in the Earth system and
                                                                                                  407
                                            partly exceed effect thresholds in marine mammals; or the global spread of
                                            highly persistent per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFASs). Such examples
                                            underscore the need for strict testing before large-volume production and use.
                                            Other aspects that require consideration are the environmental transport and
                                            transformation of novel entities which may result in transformation products
                                            that are more toxic than the original chemicals. An example is a tire wear-de-
                                            rived oxidation product of the additive 6PPD-quinone, which was found to
                                                                                     408
                                            induce acute mortality in coho salmon and other fish species of commercial,
                                                                                 409
                                            cultural and ecological importance.

             including production of plastics and chemicals, use of pesticides, emissions of per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances
             (PFASs), and impacts such as antimicrobial resistance (AMR).

             KEY TAKEAWAY: Production, use, release and impacts of many novel entities such as plastics, pesticides, PFASs and
             others are increasing, posing threats to the Earth system as well as to environmental and human health.

             Persistent organic pollutants like PCBs, PFASs and
             anthropogenic materials including (micro)plastics
             and their associated chemicals pose risks of irre-
             versible environmental effects with potential Earth
             system relevance because they are globally distrib-
             uted and cannot readily be removed from the Earth
                     176
             system. Impacts may also emerge through bio-
             logical and evolutionary responses. For example,
             the overuse of antimicrobial agents can promote
             antimicrobial resistance (AMR), which can spread
             among microbial communities through horizontal
             gene transfer. These resistance traits can persist

global aspirations to be achieved by the year 2030,   with the stability of the Earth system, as adopted

on this foundation while placing greater empha-       ensure that future development goals are not only
sis on the biophysical guardrails that make long-     socially ambitious, but also physically achievable
term development possible. Planetary Boundaries       on a finite planet.




         The science is clear:
         We have pushed the planet far beyond its Safe Operating
         Space. Despite some positive developments, our current
         efforts are far from sufficient. The window of opportuni-
         ty to act and avoid irreversible change is narrowing, but it
         is still open. Through urgent and decisive action, we can
         change our trajectory towards sustainability. Returning to
         the Safe Operating Space is the defining task for a stable
         and livable future.
